Once that starts, I just can't seem to shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying feel bigger than just the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, the house is what started it, but now it's just... everything. Like I said, it bleeds into my teaching, the robotics club, my family. It's not just about the remodeling anymore. It's like the worry just attached itself to every part of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned earlier, it's been about seven months now. And... yeah, it really is most days. Honestly, it's pretty much every single day since that pipe burst and the remodeling threw the house into chaos. It just hasn't let up since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It seems like my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something at the house, and then suddenly I'm stressing over school work—wondering if my lesson plans are slipping, or if the kids in the robotics club are falling behind. And then I start thinking about whether I'm disappointing my wife and family by just... not keeping everything under control. It's just a lot of different things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. It's been going on for about seven months now. That's when we had a pipe burst at our place, and the remodeling just... it threw the whole house into chaos. I guess it all really started then.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, I can tell myself to stop and just focus on the class right in front of me, but... it doesn't really work. Just one small thought popping into my head—like remembering a late invoice for the house or noticing a student falling behind—and it sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. Once that starts, I just can't seem to shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying feel bigger than just the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, the house is what started it, but now it's just... everything. Like I said, it bleeds into my teaching, the robotics club, my family. It's not just about the remodeling anymore. It's like the worry just attached itself to every part of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned earlier, it's been about seven months now. And... yeah, it really is most days. Honestly, it's pretty much every single day since that pipe burst and the remodeling threw the house into chaos. It just hasn't let up since then.\n\nDoctor: If the problems with your house were fully resolved, do you think this level of worry would still be there?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'd like to think that if the house was fixed, I'd go back to normal. But... since the worry has spread to my teaching and everything else, I'm just not sure anymore. It feels like my brain is just stuck in this gear now, so even if the house was done, I might just find something else to stress about.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. At work, I've started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading, which isn't like me. And outside of school... well, I usually help manage some things for the local community theater, but I actually had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with how my head's been.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to the house-remodeling stress, or does it now feel separate from that?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the house, definitely. But now... I'm really not sure. Like I said, it's affecting my teaching and the theater stuff, which has nothing to do with the remodel. So I guess it feels like it's taken on a life of its own at this point. It's not just about the house anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Um... I haven't really noticed myself snapping at anyone or getting angry more often. It's more just... I'm constantly worried and overwhelmed by everything, rather than feeling irritable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my sleep has actually been okay. I might lay there for a few minutes thinking about what I have to do for school the next day, but I haven't had any real trouble falling asleep or staying asleep. So, no, sleep hasn't been an issue for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It seems like my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something at the house, and then suddenly I'm stressing over school work—wondering if my lesson plans are slipping, or if the kids in the robotics club are falling behind. It's just a lot of different things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. It's been going on for about seven months now. It just hasn't let up since then.\n\nDoctor: If the problems with your house were fully resolved, do you think this level of worry would still be there?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'd like to think that if the house was fixed, I'd go back to normal. But... since the worry has spread to my teaching and everything else, I'm just not sure anymore. It feels like my brain is just stuck in this gear now, so even if the house was done, I might just find something else to stress about.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. At work, I've started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading, which isn't like me. And outside of school... well, I usually help manage some things for the local community theater, but I actually had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with how my head's been.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to the house-remodeling stress, or does it now feel separate from that?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the house, definitely. But now... I'm really not sure. Like I said, it's affecting my teaching and the theater stuff, which has nothing to do with the remodel. So I guess it feels like it's taken on a life of its own at this point. It's not just about the house anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Um... I haven't really noticed myself snapping at anyone or getting angry more often. It's more just... I'm constantly worried and overwhelmed by everything, rather than feeling irritable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my sleep has actually been okay. I might lay there for a few minutes thinking about what I have to do for school the next day, but I haven't had any real trouble falling asleep or staying asleep. So, no, sleep hasn't been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I can still focus on my lesson plans and grade papers without too much trouble. I mean, my mind is definitely busy with all the worrying, but when I need to sit down and concentrate on my work or the theater stuff, I can still do it. So, no real issues there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily fatigued or worn out more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. By the time the last bell rings at school, I just feel completely wrung out. It's... it's a kind of exhaustion that a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday doesn't really seem to fix anymore. Even just sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your anxiety still feel mostly centered on the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: No, not just the house anymore. I mean, it's definitely still there, but my mind just keeps jumping around now. I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the remodel, and then right away I'm stressing about whether my lesson plans are slipping, or if the robotics kids are falling behind. And... it just kind of snowballs into worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and family by not keeping everything under control. So it's really all over the place, just a constant loop of all these different things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, to me, it feels like these are all things I actually need to be concerned about. The house is a big financial thing, and my students' progress is important. So, I don't know if the worry is more than it should be... it just feels like there's always something real that needs my attention, even if it is exhausting to deal with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It kind of feels like I'm constantly bracing for bad news, even when I'm just sitting at my desk at school or trying to relax and watch TV at night. It's just this constant tension, and I figured I needed to finally talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It seems like my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something at the house, and then suddenly I'm stressing over school work—wondering if my lesson plans are slipping, or if the kids in the robotics club are falling behind. And then I start thinking about whether I'm disappointing my wife and family by just... not keeping everything under control. It's just a lot of different things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. It's been going on for about seven months now. That's when we had a pipe burst at our place, and the remodeling just... it threw the whole house into chaos. I guess it all really started then.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, I can tell myself to stop and just focus on the class right in front of me, but... it doesn't really work. Just one small thought popping into my head—like remembering a late invoice for the house or noticing a student falling behind—and it sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. Once that starts, I just can't seem to shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying feel bigger than just the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, the house is what started it, but now it's just... everything. Like I said, it bleeds into my teaching, the robotics club, my family. It's not just about the remodeling anymore. It's like the worry just attached itself to every part of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned earlier, it's been about seven months now. And... yeah, it really is most days. Honestly, it's pretty much every single day since that pipe burst and the remodeling threw the house into chaos. It just hasn't let up since then.\n\nDoctor: If the problems with your house were fully resolved, do you think this level of worry would still be there?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'd like to think that if the house was fixed, I'd go back to normal. But... since the worry has spread to my teaching and everything else, I'm just not sure anymore. It feels like my brain is just stuck in this gear now, so even if the house was done, I might just find something else to stress about.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. At work, I've started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading, which isn't like me. And outside of school... well, I usually help manage some things for the local community theater, but I actually had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with how my head's been.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to the house-remodeling stress, or does it now feel separate from that?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the house, definitely. But now... I'm really not sure. Like I said, it's affecting my teaching and the theater stuff, which has nothing to do with the remodel. So I guess it feels like it's taken on a life of its own at this point. It's not just about the house anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Um... I'm constantly worried and overwhelmed by everything, rather than feeling irritable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my sleep has actually been okay. I might lay there for a few minutes thinking about what I have to do for school the next day, but I haven't had any real trouble falling asleep or staying asleep. So, no, sleep hasn't been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I can still focus on my lesson plans and grade papers without too much trouble. I mean, my mind is definitely busy with all the worrying, but when I need to sit down and concentrate on my work or the theater stuff, I can still do it. So, no real issues there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily fatigued or worn out more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. By the time the last bell rings at school, I just feel completely wrung out. It's... it's a kind of exhaustion that a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday doesn't really seem to fix anymore. Even just sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your anxiety still feel mostly centered on the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: No, not just the house anymore. I mean, it's definitely still there, but my mind just keeps jumping around now. I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the remodel, and then right away I'm stressing about whether my lesson plans are slipping, or if the robotics kids are falling behind. And... it just kind of snowballs into worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and family by not keeping everything under control. So it's really all over the place, just a constant loop of all these different things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, to me, it feels like these are all things I actually need to be concerned about. The house is a big financial thing, and my students' progress is important. So, I don't know if the worry is more than it should be... it just feels like there's always something real that needs my attention, even if it is exhausting to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry more than other people would in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I don't really talk to other people about how much they stress over this kind of stuff. I mean... maybe? My wife seems a bit more relaxed about the house remodel than I am, but I really couldn't say if I worry more than another guy in my exact shoes would. I just know how it feels to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with how my head's been.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to the house-remodeling stress, or does it now feel separate from that?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the house, definitely. But now... I'm really not sure. Like I said, it's affecting my teaching and the theater stuff, which has nothing to do with the remodel. So I guess it feels like it's taken on a life of its own at this point. It's not just about the house anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Um... I haven't really noticed myself snapping at anyone or getting angry more often. It's more just... I'm constantly worried and overwhelmed by everything, rather than feeling irritable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my sleep has actually been okay. I might lay there for a few minutes thinking about what I have to do for school the next day, but I haven't had any real trouble falling asleep or staying asleep. So, no, sleep hasn't been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I can still focus on my lesson plans and grade papers without too much trouble. I mean, my mind is definitely busy with all the worrying, but when I need to sit down and concentrate on my work or the theater stuff, I can still do it. So, no real issues there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily fatigued or worn out more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. By the time the last bell rings at school, I just feel completely wrung out. It's... it's a kind of exhaustion that a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday doesn't really seem to fix anymore. Even just sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your anxiety still feel mostly centered on the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: No, not just the house anymore. I mean, it's definitely still there, but my mind just keeps jumping around now. I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the remodel, and then right away I'm stressing about whether my lesson plans are slipping, or if the robotics kids are falling behind. And... it just kind of snowballs into worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and family by not keeping everything under control. So it's really all over the place, just a constant loop of all these different things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, to me, it feels like these are all things I actually need to be concerned about. The house is a big financial thing, and my students' progress is important. So, I don't know if the worry is more than it should be... it just feels like there's always something real that needs my attention, even if it is exhausting to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry more than other people would in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I don't really talk to other people about how much they stress over this kind of stuff. I mean... maybe? My wife seems a bit more relaxed about the house remodel than I am, but I really couldn't say if I worry more than another guy in my exact shoes would. I just know how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any days when the house situation felt under control, and if so, did your anxiety ease up then?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. Even on the days when the contractors give us a good update or something gets finished and I think, okay, the house is fine for today... I don't actually feel any better. My brain just immediately finds something else. If it's not the house, I start stressing about the upcoming robotics competition, or I worry about some paperwork I haven't finished at school. It never really stops, it just shifts to a different topic.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

So, I don't know if the worry is more than it should be... it just feels like there's always something real that needs my attention, even if it is exhausting to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry more than other people would in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I don't really talk to other people about how much they stress over this kind of stuff. I mean... maybe? My wife seems a bit more relaxed about the house remodel than I am, but I really couldn't say if I worry more than another guy in my exact shoes would. I just know how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any days when the house situation felt under control, and if so, did your anxiety ease up then?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. Even on the days when the contractors give us a good update or something gets finished and I think, okay, the house is fine for today... I don't actually feel any better. My brain just immediately finds something else. If it's not the house, I start stressing about the upcoming robotics competition, or I worry about some paperwork I haven't finished at school. It never really stops, it just shifts to a different topic.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is excessive?\n\nPatient: I guess I just... I don't really know. Like I said before, the things I'm worrying about are real things I have to deal with. The house, my students, the robotics club... they're all actual responsibilities. So it's hard for me to say if the worry itself is excessive, or if I just have a lot going on right now. It definitely feels overwhelming, but I don't know if the amount of worry is out of proportion to everything on my plate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, nice to meet you too. Well, I guess what finally made me come in is that my neck, my shoulders, and my jaw are just tight almost all the time now. It kind of feels like I'm constantly bracing for bad news, even when I'm just sitting at my desk at school or trying to relax and watch TV at night. It's just this constant tension, and I figured I needed to finally talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It seems like my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something at the house, and then suddenly I'm stressing over school work—wondering if my lesson plans are slipping, or if the kids in the robotics club are falling behind. And then I start thinking about whether I'm disappointing my wife and family by just... not keeping everything under control. It's just a lot of different things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. It's been going on for about seven months now. That's when we had a pipe burst at our place, and the remodeling just... it threw the whole house into chaos. I guess it all really started then.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, I can tell myself to stop and just focus on the class right in front of me, but... it doesn't really work. Just one small thought popping into my head—like remembering a late invoice for the house or noticing a student falling behind—and it sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. Once that starts, I just can't seem to shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying feel bigger than just the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. I mean, the house is what started it, but now it's just... everything. Like I said, it bleeds into my teaching, the robotics club, my family. It's not just about the remodeling anymore. It's like the worry just attached itself to every part of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned earlier, it's been about seven months now. And... yeah, it really is most days. Honestly, it's pretty much every single day since that pipe burst and the remodeling threw the house into chaos. It just hasn't let up since then.\n\nDoctor: If the problems with your house were fully resolved, do you think this level of worry would still be there?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'd like to think that if the house was fixed, I'd go back to normal. But... since the worry has spread to my teaching and everything else, I'm just not sure anymore. It feels like my brain is just stuck in this gear now, so even if the house was done, I might just find something else to stress about.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. At work, I've started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading, which isn't like me. And outside of school... well, I usually help manage some things for the local community theater, but I actually had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with how my head's been.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to the house-remodeling stress, or does it now feel separate from that?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the house, definitely. But now... I'm really not sure. Like I said, it's affecting my teaching and the theater stuff, which has nothing to do with the remodel. So I guess it feels like it's taken on a life of its own at this point. It's not just about the house anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Um... I haven't really noticed myself snapping at anyone or getting angry more often. It's more just... I'm constantly worried and overwhelmed by everything, rather than feeling irritable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my sleep has actually been okay. I might lay there for a few minutes thinking about what I have to do for school the next day, but I haven't had any real trouble falling asleep or staying asleep. So, no, sleep hasn't been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I can still focus on my lesson plans and grade papers without too much trouble. I mean, my mind is definitely busy with all the worrying, but when I need to sit down and concentrate on my work or the theater stuff, I can still do it. So, no real issues there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily fatigued or worn out more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. By the time the last bell rings at school, I just feel completely wrung out. It's... it's a kind of exhaustion that a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday doesn't really seem to fix anymore. Even just sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your anxiety still feel mostly centered on the house-remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: No, not just the house anymore. I mean, it's definitely still there, but my mind just keeps jumping around now. I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the remodel, and then right away I'm stressing about whether my lesson plans are slipping, or if the robotics kids are falling behind. And... it just kind of snowballs into worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and family by not keeping everything under control. So it's really all over the place, just a constant loop of all these different things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, to me, it feels like these are all things I actually need to be concerned about. The house is a big financial thing, and my students' progress is important. So, I don't know if the worry is more than it should be... it just feels like there's always something real that needs my attention, even if it is exhausting to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry more than other people would in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. It definitely feels overwhelming, but I don't know if the amount of worry is out of proportion to everything on my plate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is stronger than the situation really warrants?\n\nPatient: I think that's... basically what I was just trying to say. I mean, the house is what started it, but now it's just... everything. Like I said, it bleeds into my teaching, the robotics club, my family. It's not just about the remodeling anymore. It's like the worry just attached itself to every part of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned earlier, it's been about seven months now. And... yeah, it really is most days. Honestly, it's pretty much every single day since that pipe burst and the remodeling threw the house into chaos. It just hasn't let up since then.\n\nDoctor: If the problems with your house were fully resolved, do you think this level of worry would still be there?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'd like to think that if the house was fixed, I'd go back to normal. But... since the worry has spread to my teaching and everything else, I'm just not sure anymore. It feels like my brain is just stuck in this gear now, so even if the house was done, I might just find something else to stress about.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. At work, I've started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading, which isn't like me. And outside of school... well, I usually help manage some things for the local community theater, but I actually had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with how my head's been.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to the house-remodeling stress, or does it now feel separate from that?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the house, definitely. But now... I'm really not sure. Like I said, it's affecting my teaching and the theater stuff, which has nothing to do with the remodel. So I guess it feels like it's taken on a life of its own at this point. It's not just about the house anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Um... I haven't really noticed myself snapping at anyone or getting angry more often. It's more just... I'm constantly worried and overwhelmed by everything, rather than feeling irritable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my sleep has actually been okay. And... it just kind of snowballs into worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and family by not keeping everything under control. Like I said before, the things I'm worrying about are real things I have to deal with. The house, my students, the robotics club... they're all actual responsibilities. So it's hard for me to say if the worry itself is excessive, or if I just have a lot going on right now. It definitely feels overwhelming, but I don't know if the amount of worry is out of proportion to everything on my plate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is stronger than the situation really warrants?\n\nPatient: I think that's... basically what I was just trying to say. I really don't know. It's hard to measure what a situation actually warrants, you know? Teaching, the house, the club... they're all pretty demanding. So I couldn't tell you for sure if my reaction is stronger than it should be. I just know I'm stressed out by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel pretty wound up most of the day, honestly. It's like I'm just... waiting for something else to go wrong. I actually catch myself pacing around the classroom a lot while the students are working. And between periods, I'm constantly checking my phone to see if there are any new messages from the contractor. I just have a really hard time settling down."
